It’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble explaining things or feeling like your words are jumping around in situations outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yes, it happens outside of work too. If I’m trying to explain something simple, my words can kind of jump around and I lose the thread. It’s like I start with one point, then I’m talking about something else, and people don’t always follow what I mean.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre these difficulties with explaining things and your words jumping around causing you a lot of distress or significantly impacting your daily life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / first_line_supervisor_of_retail_sales_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m not trusted to run busy weekend shifts anymore, and after the performance review my boss warned me I could be suspended if things don’t improve. At home, my wife has had to cover commitments I used to handle because people started getting uncomfortable or confused around me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. It’s embarrassing, and it’s stressful.\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any alcohol or drugs around the time you notice your words jumping around?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear connection with drinking or drugs. It seems to happen in regular situations too, not like only after I’ve used anything.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo these episodes of your words jumping around happen all the time, or do they come and go?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / first_line_supervisor_of_retail_sales_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Thinking_Speech\nmanifestation: \"I came in because when I try to explain inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, my words start jumping from trucks to Thursdays to the church grill to my boss’s tone, and people look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. It’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble explaining things or feeling like your words are jumping around in situations outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yes, it happens outside of work too. If I’m trying to explain something simple, my words can kind of jump around and I lose the thread. It’s like I start with one point, then I’m talking about something else, and people don’t always follow what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with explaining things and your words jumping around causing you a lot of distress or significantly impacting your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s affecting things pretty badly. At work they don’t really trust me to run the busy weekend shifts anymore, and after my last performance review my boss said I could be suspended if I don’t get it together.\n\nAt home too, my wife has had to take over things I used to handle, because people get confused or uncomfortable when I’m trying to talk through plans or commitments. It’s embarrassing, and it’s stressful.\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any alcohol or drugs around the time you notice your words jumping around?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear connection with drinking or drugs. It seems to happen in regular situations too, not like only after I’ve used anything.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of your words jumping around happen all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: It comes and goes. It’s not every single minute, but it happens enough that I notice it. Like when I’m trying to explain inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, I’ll start talking and suddenly I’m jumping from the delivery trucks to Thursday, then something about the church grill, then my boss’s tone. People look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice any changes in your mood, such as feeling anxious or irritable, when your words are jumping around?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I do get frustrated when it happens, because I can tell people aren’t following me, but I haven’t noticed a clear mood change that comes first. It’s more like the stress comes after I realize I’m not making sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear or see things that other people don't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t noticed seeing things that aren’t there or hearing voices like that. Sometimes I’m confused in a conversation, but not really hearing or seeing things other people don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life when you used alcohol or drugs heavily?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of. I’ve had drinks here and there, but I wouldn’t say there was ever a time I was using alcohol or drugs heavily.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your thoughts seemed completely disconnected from reality?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely disconnected from reality. It’s more that when I’m trying to explain something normal, like inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, my words start jumping around. I’ll go from talking about trucks, to Thursday, to some church grill, to my boss’s tone, and then people are looking at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt like you were losing touch with reality?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it losing touch with reality exactly. It’s more like, when I try to explain something at work—inventory numbers or the weekend schedule—my words don’t stay in order. I’ll jump from trucks to Thursday to the church grill to my boss’s tone, and then people just look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s embarrassing, and it’s stressful.\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any alcohol or drugs around the time you notice your words jumping around?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear connection with drinking or drugs. It seems to happen in regular situations too, not like only after I’ve used anything.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of your words jumping around happen all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: It comes and goes. It’s not every single minute, but it happens enough that I notice it. Like when I’m trying to explain inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, I’ll start talking and suddenly I’m jumping from the delivery trucks to Thursday, then something about the church grill, then my boss’s tone. People look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice any changes in your mood, such as feeling anxious or irritable, when your words are jumping around?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I do get frustrated when it happens, because I can tell people aren’t following me, but I haven’t noticed a clear mood change that comes first. It’s more like the stress comes after I realize I’m not making sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear or see things that other people don't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t noticed seeing things that aren’t there or hearing voices like that. Sometimes I’m confused in a conversation, but not really hearing or seeing things other people don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life when you used alcohol or drugs heavily?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of. I’ve had drinks here and there, but I wouldn’t say there was ever a time I was using alcohol or drugs heavily.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your thoughts seemed completely disconnected from reality?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely disconnected from reality. It’s more that when I’m trying to explain something normal, like inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, my words start jumping around. I’ll go from talking about trucks, to Thursday, to some church grill, to my boss’s tone, and then people are looking at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt like you were losing touch with reality?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it losing touch with reality exactly. It’s more like, when I try to explain something at work—inventory numbers or the weekend schedule—my words don’t stay in order. I’ll jump from trucks to Thursday to the church grill to my boss’s tone, and then people just look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are jumping around in the same way that your words are when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It’s like my thoughts don’t stay in one lane, and then my words come out that way too. I’ll be trying to talk about inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, and suddenly I’m onto trucks, then Thursday, then the church grill, then my boss’s tone. I can tell people are trying to follow me, but they look lost.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant change in your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain relationships since your words started jumping around\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s affected things pretty clearly. At work, they don’t really trust me to run the busy weekend shifts anymore, and after my last performance review my boss said I could be suspended if I don’t get it together.\n\nAt home it’s been hard too. My wife has had to take over commitments I used to handle, because people get uncomfortable or confused when I’m trying to talk things through. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have times when your thoughts and words are completely normal and organized, without any jumping around?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes, I can sound normal for a while. But when I have to explain something more involved, like inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, that’s when it starts happening. I’ll mean to stay on one point, and then I’m off talking about trucks, Thursdays, the church grill, or my boss’s tone, and people look like they can’t follow me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are as disorganized as your words when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yes. It feels like it starts in my head first, not just my mouth. I’m trying to explain something simple, like inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, and my thoughts jump from one thing to another — trucks, then Thursday, then the church grill, then something about my boss’s tone. By the time I’m talking, people are looking at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you have times when your thoughts and words are completely normal and organized for an entire day, without any jumping around?\n\nPatient: Not really for a whole day, no. I can have stretches where I’m fine, but if I have to explain something like the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, it starts jumping around again. I’ll go from trucks to Thursday to the church grill or my boss’s tone, and then people look at me like they’ve lost track of what I’m saying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s affecting things pretty badly. If I’m trying to explain something simple, my words can kind of jump around and I lose the thread. It’s like I start with one point, then I’m talking about something else, and people don’t always follow what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with explaining things and your words jumping around causing you a lot of distress or significantly impacting your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s affecting things pretty badly. At work they don’t really trust me to run the busy weekend shifts anymore, and after my last performance review my boss said I could be suspended if I don’t get it together.\n\nAt home too, my wife has had to take over things I used to handle, because people get confused or uncomfortable when I’m trying to talk through plans or commitments. I haven’t noticed a clear connection with drinking or drugs. It seems to happen in regular situations too, not like only after I’ve used anything.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of your words jumping around happen all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: It comes and goes. It’s not every single minute, but it happens enough that I notice it. People look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice any changes in your mood, such as feeling anxious or irritable, when your words are jumping around?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I do get frustrated when it happens, because I can tell people aren’t following me, but I haven’t noticed a clear mood change that comes first. It’s more like the stress comes after I realize I’m not making sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear or see things that other people don't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t noticed seeing things that aren’t there or hearing voices like that. Sometimes I’m confused in a conversation, but not really hearing or seeing things other people don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life when you used alcohol or drugs heavily?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of. I’ve had drinks here and there, but I wouldn’t say there was ever a time I was using alcohol or drugs heavily.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your thoughts seemed completely disconnected from reality?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely disconnected from reality. It’s more that when I’m trying to explain something normal, like inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, my words start jumping around. I’ll go from talking about trucks, to Thursday, to some church grill, to my boss’s tone, and then people are looking at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt like you were losing touch with reality?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it losing touch with reality exactly. It’s more like, when I try to explain something at work—inventory numbers or the weekend schedule—my words don’t stay in order. I’ll jump from trucks to Thursday to the church grill to my boss’s tone, and then people just look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are jumping around in the same way that your words are when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It’s like my thoughts don’t stay in one lane, and then my words come out that way too. I’ll mean to stay on one point, and then I’m off talking about trucks, Thursdays, the church grill, or my boss’s tone, and people look like they can’t follow me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are as disorganized as your words when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’m trying to explain something simple, like inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, and my thoughts jump from one thing to another — trucks, then Thursday, then the church grill, then something about my boss’s tone. By the time I’m talking, people are looking at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you have times when your thoughts and words are completely normal and organized for an entire day, without any jumping around?\n\nPatient: Not really for a whole day, no.
